Strategic plan · Dynamiq Engineering Standard v1.0, Appendix C.1

Gabay Phase Plan

A phase-by-phase route from an empty repository to a released indoor wayfinding app for Philippine malls. It covers nine product-owner decisions, each with sourced evidence and a recommendation. Six were ruled on 2026-09-25, and D4 was partly ruled the same day. D4c, D4d, D8 and D9 are still open. On 2026-09-28 the prototype became the product (L34). It follows the gates, while throwaway D7 spikes gather the evidence for D4d.

Context & scope

Gabay is a customer-facing indoor wayfinding app for shopping malls in the Philippines. Mall-operator companies are the paying clients, and shoppers use the app directly.

  • Positioning: BLE beacons.
  • Maps: each client's admins draw their own in a web editor built into Gabay.
  • Money: none in the MVP. Future loyalty or discount features (D1) would bring it in, and decimal.js and DECIMAL(18,4) apply from then on.
  • Shopper accounts: optional. Shoppers use the app anonymously, and a visible sign-in is never forced (D1).
  • Pilot client: none yet.
  • Team: one developer working with Claude.

This draft adds two amendment passes to draft 0.1:

  • A compliance pass resolving fifteen audit findings against the standard.
  • A content pass closing six gaps in the positioning design.
  • A decision pass (0.4) recording the product owner's rulings on D1–D3 and D5–D7. See the decision log.
Shopper app
Mobile · Android + iOS

Field testing starts on Android devices. The code is portable to iOS from day one, and an iOS build compiles in CI on every PR. 0.47 (L103): Android 7.0+ and iOS 15+, both released at the pilot; phones and tablets.

Admin dashboard
Flutter Web

The back office and map editor are a first-class surface. The standard's web rules apply to them in full.

Evidence base
15 research passes

Seven on 2026-09-24; on 2026-09-25, two for D6 and four for D4; on 2026-09-28, two for outdoor positioning and beacon placement. Sources are linked inline, and anything unconfirmed is marked UNVERIFIED.

You asked to make these calls yourself. Each row links to its evidence and trade-offs. Nothing is built until they are settled: D1–D8 before the P0 gate, and D9 before any beacon purchase. Every ruling is traced in the decision log. 0.7: the design rulings L25–L42 are summarised in the visualization review, routing, editor and planner design and roles sections. Decisions

D1Shopper accounts

Ruled 2026-09-25

Anonymous, optional login, or login required?

Evidence

  • Apple 5.1.1(v): "If your app doesn't include significant account-based features, let people use it without a login." Apps that support account creation must also offer in-app deletion. Apple guidelines
  • Google Play: apps with accounts need both an in-app and a web deletion path. Play policy
  • Sign in with Apple (4.8): required as soon as Google or Facebook login is offered.
  • Forced sign-up: 19% of shoppers abandon checkout when forced to create an account (Baymard). Login walls are "rarely justified", and mobile users delete the app instead of registering (NN/g, 2014).
  • Retention: shopping apps keep about 5% of users at day 30 (AppsFlyer/Adjust via UXCam, secondary source).
OptionStrengthsWeaknesses
A. Anonymous onlyNo login friction. No deletion flows and no Sign in with Apple. Minimal personal data under the Data Privacy Act. Position stays on the deviceNo favourites across devices. The only way to re-engage is device push opt-in. Weaker per-user analytics
B. Anonymous first, optional login laterKeeps A's frictionless start. Login arrives with a feature that needs it (saved places, loyalty)Adds deletion flows on both stores, plus Sign in with Apple if social login is offered. Two kinds of user to support
C. Login requiredRichest data. Easiest to re-engageLikely conflicts with Apple 5.1.1(v). Largest drop-off and privacy surface
Recommendation (draft 0.3)

A for the MVP, designed so B can be added. Keep admin identity separate from shoppers from day one. Revisit B only when a mall client asks for a feature that needs an account.

Ruling · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.4

B, with the login in the MVP: "the user can use it anonymously but it has a login functionality in which the system do not forces the user to login but also it doesnt makes the login option hidden from the user". Login "will be essential in the future in terms of other promos like loyalty, discount, etc". This differs from the recommendation. What follows from it:

  • Anonymous use stays complete. Every P0 feature works without signing in, which keeps Apple 5.1.1(v) satisfied.
  • Account deletion in the app (Apple) and on the web (Google Play), shipped with the login.
  • D1b, the sign-in method, is decided in P0 when the PRD §6 auth section is written. If Google or Facebook login is offered, Apple 4.8 applies. The rule was revised in 2024, so re-verify its current wording then UNVERIFIED. L109 rules D1b: email, Google and Sign in with Apple; 4.8's wording was re-read on 2026-10-07.
  • Shopper identity is separate from admin identity: its own tables, and its own routes outside the GET-only public router.
  • Invariant 4 is unchanged. Signing in never attaches position to an account.
  • More personal data under the Data Privacy Act: the privacy notice covers account data, and the NPC registration check at P5 includes it.
  • Money: when discounts arrive, the §3.9 money rules apply in full.

D2Offline strategy

Ruled 2026-09-25

Offline-first, online-only, or a hybrid?

Evidence

  • Concrete: 203 mm of reinforced concrete weakens the signal by about 31 dB (NIST, cited by UCtel, secondary source).
  • PH malls: at Festival Mall "some dead spots remain" after Globe's upgrade (InsiderPH, 2026-08-11). SM's free Wi-Fi is capped at 2 hours (SM), and apps fail behind captive portals.
  • Precedent: Mappedin computes directions on the device without a connection. Situm's viewer "can work fully offline" (Situm).
  • Package size: 0.3–3 MB per venue, gzipped UNVERIFIED. D7 Spike 2 measures it.
OptionStrengthsWeaknesses
Online-onlySimplest. Always fresh. No on-device DBFails in basements, dead spots and captive portals, which is exactly where people need directions
Offline-firstWorks everywhere. Instant routingRisk of stale data. Storage on low-end phones. Versioning complexity. Needs an on-device store (D6)
HybridWorks offline, and checks freshness when online. One immutable package per venue version keeps the app, the server and the tests in agreementNeeds publishing and versioning machinery, and cache eviction. A refresh can miss a same-day change
Recommendation (draft 0.3)

Hybrid. Each published venue version becomes an immutable package: levels, units, graph, directory, logos, boundary and beacon registry.

  • The app downloads the package when a venue is picked, and checks it by ETag or version whenever it's online.
  • It keeps the previous version until the new one is verified.
  • Routing and search run on the device.
  • A captive portal is treated as offline.
Ruling · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.4

Hybrid, as recommended. The package's on-device form is set by D6.

D3MVP scope

Ruled 2026-09-25

Features are ranked by evidence, and by cost given the other decisions.

FeatureEvidenceCostTier
Directory & searchProduct search was a download driver for 56.7% of app users (US survey, PYMNTS/LISNR 2019, eMarketer)Low. No beacons. Fewest privacy issuesP0
Multi-floor routingCore differentiator. The evidence that a live blue dot adds value comes only from vendors (Mappedin); independent evidence UNVERIFIED. Dubai Mall logged 2.8M wayfinding sessions in about 2 years (case study)Medium–high. Graph, editor, routing engineP0
Step-free routesPSA 2020: 1.47M people with walking difficulty and 9.22M seniors (60+). Malls are already bound by BP 344Very low once the graph existsP0
Aggregated analyticsWhat mall clients pay for; evidence from vendors only (V-Count). Must be truly anonymised (NPC Advisory 2025-02)Medium. Minimum-count thresholds, no raw tracesP1
Promos & pushAbout 40% of consumers resist retailer apps because they already have too many (Signifyd/PYMNTS). Targeting individuals triggers NPC registration (Circular 2022-04)Medium. Push infrastructure plus consentP3

Adoption hedge, to consider: QR codes on mall signage that open a web directory. No iOS browser supports Web Bluetooth (caniuse), so the web version has no blue dot. Proposed as a P2 entry.

Recommendation (draft 0.3)

P0: directory and search, multi-floor routing, step-free routes. P1: aggregated analytics. P2/P3: promos and push. Open item C4: this last row needs a single tier.

Ruling · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.4

As recommended, with promos and push at P3. P0: directory and search, multi-floor routing, step-free routes. P1: aggregated analytics. P3: promos and push, which gives PRD §6.12 the Low priority and closes C4. D1 ties loyalty and discounts to the future shopper login, so they arrive alongside promos. The QR web-directory hedge stays a proposed P2 entry.

D4Positioning rollout

BLE confirmedD4a · D4b ruled 2026-09-25D4c · D4d ruled 2026-10-07 (L114)

Status, 2026-09-25 (0.5) New in 0.5: the product owner brought the promised task, a live location from the moment of entry. D4 is now split into four parts:

  • D4a (entry detection) and D4b (engine) are ruled.
  • D4c (build or buy) is saved as proposals for Allan and Raphael.
  • D4d (where the live dot sits in the roadmap) is kept open.
  • L114 (2026-10-07): D4c is ruled in-house and D4d a P0 live dot with the fallback.

See Live location from entry below. The 0.3 material that follows is kept for audit.

Status, 2026-09-25 New in 0.4: the product owner deferred the staging: "this is yet to be confirmed through analysis and discussion related to the task i will prompt you later". When that task arrives, the next pass brings staging proposals with evidence of efficiency and of real-world deployments that match actual mall scenarios. D8 and D9 are held with D4, because both build on the staging. The evidence and recommendation below are kept unchanged as input.

BLE beacons are settled. What's open is how to stage them so the MVP doesn't wait on hardware installed at a mall we don't yet have.

Evidence

  • Accuracy: RSSI trilateration alone gave 5–10 m error in a 2,400 m² store with 81 beacons (PMC, 2019).
  • MLoc (MobiCom'22, project) reports a 2.4 m median across 35 malls. That combines BLE, geomagnetic fingerprints surveyed by paid collectors, and step-based dead reckoning in a particle filter.
    • Its BLE-only first fix was a 4.1 m median.
    • It degraded to 4.1 m after a year without maintenance.
    • It was tested only on Huawei, Xiaomi, Oppo and iPhone.
    • The 2.4 m figure therefore describes stage 4, which Gabay excludes, not stage 3.
    • Vendors claim 1–4 m; vendor figures aren't evidence.
  • Hardware: 1 beacon per 300–400 m² (Situm) to 1 per 74–149 m² (Pointr). $5–35 a unit, 2–6 years of battery. A 100,000 m² mall needs 250–1,350 beacons, roughly $2k–47k in hardware (our arithmetic). Economics are in D8.
  • iOS: ranging needs CoreLocation, and "was never meant to work in the background" (Apple forums, 2025).
  • Android: at most 5 scan starts per 30 s, and going over silently returns nothing (Punch Through). OEM power savers break detection (AltBeacon).
  • PH market: Transsion 33%, Xiaomi 22% (Omdia Q2 2026). Android is 85.6% (StatCounter). Transsion and realme phones generally lack a barometer (epey).
  • Plugins:
    • flutter_beacon: abandoned.
    • dchs_flutter_beacon: maintained. Uses CoreLocation on iOS and AltBeacon on Android.
    • flutter_blue_plus: can't see iBeacons on iOS, and needs a paid licence for for-profit use.
    • situm_flutter: vendor SDK, licence cost UNVERIFIED.
  • History: retail beacon adoption stalled over low installs, battery upkeep and spam (9to5Mac, 2018). Duon is piloting at SM North EDSA (duon.ph); its technology is UNVERIFIED.
OptionStrengthsWeaknesses
A. BLE blue dot from day oneMatches the vision immediatelyThe MVP is blocked until beacons are installed at a mall we don't have. Fingerprinting and tuning per device model. Highest risk on the dominant PH phones
B. Staged: QR anchors → BLE proximity → BLE + step countingStage 1 works on every phone UNVERIFIED (0.5: tagged per C12a; the Spike 1 QR-scan test checks it) with no hardware, so it can be demoed and sold first. Stage 2 (snap to the nearest beacon, no fingerprinting) targets 3–5 m off-peak; accuracy at peak crowd density is unmeasured. Stage 3 (BLE plus step counting, fused without a survey): the best independent result is 1.19 m mean in a 5,760 m² building with 39 beacons (Pan et al. 2025); on PH budget phones it is UNVERIFIEDStage 1 has no live blue dot. None of six sampled Tecno, Infinix, itel and Redmi models lists a gyroscope (GSMArena). Stage 3's heading then depends on Android's geomagnetic rotation vector, whose accuracy Google calls "lower" (Android). Stage 3 gets no pipeline entry until the pilot measurement exists
C. Buy a vendor SDKProven 1–4 m, maintainedLicence cost UNVERIFIED, vendor lock-in. Vendor schemes resolve beacon IDs in the vendor's cloud (see D9)

Stage 4 (surveyed fingerprinting) is excluded. Its cost and the reasons are recorded in PRD §2.

iOS portability, although field tests are Android-only

  • iOS sees iBeacons only through CoreLocation, while Android uses AltBeacon-style scanning. The difference stays behind PositionSource.
  • Beacons must advertise iBeacon format (or both iBeacon and Eddystone). Eddystone is visible to iOS only in the foreground.
  • Permission flows are written for both platforms from the start.
Recommendation (draft 0.3, superseded in part by D4a and D4b)

B, with PositionSource designed in Phase 0 so C stays open.

  • Use dchs_flutter_beacon or a thin platform channel, never flutter_blue_plus.
  • Navigation is a foreground session.
  • No numeric accuracy promise in product copy until the pilot's peak and off-peak measurement exists (PRD §7). The internal target for stage 2 is 3–5 m off-peak.
  • Draw an uncertainty circle, and size geofences at 6 m or more.
  • Detect floor changes from escalator and elevator anchors and per-floor beacon grouping. Use the barometer only where the phone has one.

Live location from entry (0.5) New in 0.5

The product owner's goal (2026-09-25): "the moment the user enters that mall, that individual must see their live location and follow the direction the moment the move and that will serve as the starting point for every search of destination." Four research passes ran on 2026-09-25: entry detection, continuous positioning, real deployments, and efficiency enhancements.

Evidence: detecting entry

  • App open: fused location reaches "20–50 m" with Wi-Fi, and "as small as 5 meters" where indoor location is available. That's enough to pick the mall, but it gives no floor (Android geofencing docs). The first beacon fix comes an estimated 1–3 s after scanning starts UNVERIFIED.
  • App closed, iOS: region monitoring can relaunch the app only with Always authorization. When In Use "Launches a terminated app automatically: No" (Apple). The limit is 20 conditions, and the app gets about 10 s when woken.
  • App closed, Android:
    • Geofencing takes "about 2-3 minutes on average… (up to 6 minutes)" (Android geofencing docs).
    • Background BLE needs ACCESS_BACKGROUND_LOCATION. A location foreground service can't be started from the background without it (Android).
    • On dontkillmyapp (community-run), Xiaomi and Samsung score 5/5 for killing background apps, and Tecno and realme 3/5.
  • Store policy:
    • Google Play background location must be "relevant to the core functionality", and needs a declaration and a video. It lists turn-by-turn navigation as something foreground access can do (Play).
    • A new Play precise-location declaration is mandatory from 2027-01-27 for all apps requesting precise location (Play).
    • Apple guideline 5.1.1(iv): "provide alternative solutions for users who don't grant consent" (Apple).
  • Precedent: Google shut down beacon notifications in 2018 over "spammy notifications" (Google).
Entry optionTime to first locationPH budget AndroidPolicy riskRule conflict
A. App-open auto-detectAbout 1–3 s after scanning starts UNVERIFIEDHighLowNone
B. A + opt-in background arrival alertUndocumented on either platformLow to medium (OEM app killers)High (Play background location; Always on iOS)Narrows invariant 5
C. Android geofencing2–3 min average, up to 6 minLowHighSame as B

Evidence: a dot that follows movement

  • BLE only: "<2.6 m 95% of the time" at 1 beacon per 30 m², and "<4.8 m" at 1 per 100 m² (Faragher & Harle, IEEE JSAC 2015). Pan et al. 2025 measured a 2.01 m mean at 1 per 148 m² using BLE alone, 1.51 m with step counting added, and 1.19 m with the floor plan added. The phone was a flagship with a gyroscope (arXiv).
  • No gyroscope: "the lack of gyro readings makes the heading estimation very inaccurate, especially in areas with high concentrations of metal". A single metal object can disturb the compass by up to 30° over 5–15 m. Particle filters "often fail to converge" on gyro-free data. MapCraft's walkway-constrained matcher reached 1.14–1.83 m RMS at 10 ms per step on a Nexus 4 (MapCraft, IPSN 2014).
  • Floors without a barometer: per-floor beacon grouping gave 92.7% real-time floor accuracy (Sensors 2021).
  • Wi-Fi is not viable:
    • Android: "Each foreground app can scan four times in a 2-minute period" (Android).
    • iOS has no general Wi-Fi scanning API.
    • Geomagnetic fingerprinting needs a gyroscope and a venue survey (IndoorAtlas, vendor).
  • Real-mall reality check: at IPIN 2018 in the Atlantis mall, Nantes, the best on-site smartphone system had an 11.7 m third-quartile error (IPIN 2018). No accuracy figure has been published for gyro-less budget phones UNVERIFIED.
EngineAccuracy evidenceGyro-less budget phonesHardware
Nearest beaconZone level, 5–10 mYesSparse beacons
BLE trilateration + filter2.0 m mean (Pan); <2.6–4.8 m at 95% (Faragher)Yes, with per-model calibrationDense beacons
BLE + steps + walkway matching1.1–1.8 m RMS (MapCraft); 1.19 m (Pan, with gyro)No published figure; heading from the walkway graph, not the compassBeacons + walkway graph
Wi-Fi or geomagnetic<8.5 m (Wi-Fi); 1–3 m magnetic (vendor)Wi-Fi throttled; magnetic needs a gyroNone, or a survey

Evidence: real deployments (accuracy figures are vendor claims)

  • Dubai Mall: Mappedin maps with Senion BLE, updating "twice a second", and "over 2.8 million wayfinding sessions" (case study, 2019). Against 84M visitors in 2019, that is roughly 1–2% of visits (our arithmetic). Verizon bought Senion in 2021.
  • Gatwick: "around 2,000 battery-powered beacons", "+/-3m" (Pointr; TechCrunch, 2017).
  • Duon (competitor): allied with Situm for SM City North EDSA (Situm, 2024-11-06). Its App Store privacy label discloses location "used to track you across apps and websites owned by other companies" (App Store).
  • Situm privacy: by default the SDK computes a position every second and uploads it to Situm's cloud, and its docs show no way to disable the upload (Situm docs).
  • PH mall apps (SM, Ayala, Robinsons) show directories and no blue dot UNVERIFIED.
Ruling D4a · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.5

Detect the mall automatically when the app is open, plus a manual mall picker, plus a "select where you are" fallback. In the product owner's words: "first option is ideal but we can also consider the option in which the user can specify a mall they are entering too … why not make a selection available … since it is much easier although it will serve as a fallback once the live location of the user has encounter some problems". QR codes stay as an optional extra for malls that want signage.

  • On launch: fused location is checked against each venue's boundary to preselect the mall and preload its package. The shopper can also pick the mall manually from a picker at any time.
  • Then one continuous foreground beacon scan gives the live dot and the floor.
  • "Select where you are": a search-as-you-type picker of stores and landmarks, backed by FTS5 in the D6 package. The chosen place's entrance node on its floor becomes the position, drawn with an uncertainty halo. A later confident beacon fix supersedes it (for a scanned code, L101 reverses this: the scan re-seeds the live estimate at the code's spot and floor; L107 then removed QR from the product). It works on iOS and Android, and with Bluetooth or location denied, which satisfies Apple 5.1.1(iv).
  • QR: optional signage giving an exact spot and floor. The existing venue-mismatch and out-of-date handling still applies. L101: a scan is a hard reset of the live estimate and a calibration point for the phone; publish emits codes for entrances and connectors too, with placement advice.
  • Not chosen: background arrival alerts and Android geofencing. Invariant 5 (foreground only) is unchanged.
  • File the Play precise-location declaration before 2027-01-27, whatever else is chosen.
Ruling D4b · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.5

BLE + accelerometer steps + walkway-graph matching, with all four enhancement groups. The engine runs on the device, offline and in the foreground. It shares the D6 worker isolate and CSR graph with A*. The compass is a gated, weak hint, and the walkway graph decides the direction of travel. The product owner asked for enhancements "as long as it doesn't compromises the performance, security, and other critical and edge concerns".

Default route origin: the live position, when its confidence clears the threshold. Otherwise, the last selected or scanned place. Otherwise, the shopper is asked to "select where you are". Every figure above except #5 comes from flagship or older phones. Spike 1 must measure on the real budget phones: scan offload flags, the sensor list (including TYPE_ROTATION_VECTOR), per-model RSSI offsets, callback rate, the 10-min timeout and, since L101, the per-device offset a QR scan yields and the dot error in the 30 s after a scan, with and without the reset and the offset.

D5State management

  • provider 6.1.5+1 (last release 2025-08-19): Appendix A.12 gives the canonical code verbatim.
  • riverpod (flutter_riverpod 3.4.3, 2026-09-03): AsyncValue and stream providers suit Gabay's stream-heavy client.

riverpod. This is a judgement call, not a measured finding, and either choice is fully compliant. The P1 frontend setup follows whichever way it lands.

riverpod (flutter_riverpod 3.x), kept for the life of the project (§4.2). P1 sets up one ProviderScope and adapts Appendix A.12's canonical code to it once. provider leaves the pin table.

D6On-device venue-package store, and libraries the mandate doesn't name

Ruled 2026-09-25Owner note before P0 gate

The open question. §1's DB row mandates "raw parameterized SQL (no ORM)", and its deny list names "ORMs (Sequelize/Prisma/TypeORM)". Both are written about the backend and MSSQL. §1 doesn't say whether the no-ORM rule also covers on-device persistence. This must be settled before the P0 gate, because the store shapes the package format and Blueprint Part 3.

Evidence

  • drift 2.35.0 (2026-09-09, pub.dev) calls itself "a reactive persistence library for Flutter and Dart, built on top of SQLite". Neither pub.dev nor its docs call it an ORM.
  • drift's typed Dart API is plausibly ORM-like. It also runs raw SQL: "you can use methods like customSelect and customStatement … by writing the SQL manually", with ? variables and .drift SQL files (docs). Whether it counts as an ORM isn't settled by the evidence either way.
  • sqflite 2.4.4 (2026-09-10, pub.dev) is a "SQLite plugin for Flutter", with raw SQL bound through ?. Web support is experimental, which doesn't matter because the store is mobile-only.
  • What the store must hold (from D2): N immutable venue packages, the previous version kept until the new one is verified, eviction, and a small telemetry queue. Package size UNVERIFIED.
OptionStrengthsWeaknesses
drift, full typed APITyped queries, migrations, reactive streamsPlausibly an ORM under the deny list. Needs an owner ruling, and possibly EXCEPTIONS row E-01
drift, raw SQL onlyParameterized SQL written by hand, with drift's migrations and streamsNeeds a lint that bans the typed API. Still wants the owner's confirmation
sqflite + hand-written SQLComplies under either reading with no rulingHand-written migrations on the device. A second SQL dialect
Plain files + a JSON indexComplies under either reading. The package is an immutable blob by design. SimplestThe package is loaded into memory for search and routing
Recommendation (draft 0.3)

Plain files plus an index for the MVP, moving to sqflite with raw SQL if Spike 2 shows in-memory loading is too slow. Whichever you choose, route the scope question to Allan (or Raphael if Allan works on Gabay) before the P0 gate, and propose the clarification to the standard (H5). Avoid flutter_blue_plus (licence). The pinned versions are in the Blueprint Part 1 pin table.

Performance evidence (added in 0.4, two research passes on 2026-09-25) New in 0.4

The product owner asked for the option that performs best on latency, time and space complexity, concurrency and scalability, fits the stack, and has evidence behind every claim.

  • sqflite "uses the SQLite available on the platform. It does not ship/bundle any additional SQLite library" (sqflite docs).
    • AOSP's build flags don't include SQLITE_ENABLE_FTS5 or SQLITE_ENABLE_RTREE (Android.bp). OEM builds may differ UNVERIFIED.
    • Every call crosses a method channel, and "Transaction mechanism is not cross-isolate safe".
  • sqlite3 3.6.0 (2026-09-13, pub.dev) gives "direct and synchronous access to SQLite" through dart:ffi, and runs in any isolate.
    • It bundles SQLite with SQLITE_ENABLE_FTS5 and SQLITE_ENABLE_RTREE (hook docs).
    • sqlite3_flutter_libs is end-of-life (0.6.0+eol, 2026-02-15).
  • JSON decode cost: in Dart AOT, canada.json (about 2.2 MB of GeoJSON) took 31.8 ms on a desktop, "generally 1.5-3x slower than V8" (dart-lang/sdk #55522, a Dart VM engineer).
    • On a low-end phone that is an estimated 100–300 ms UNVERIFIED.
    • Flutter: "If this work takes more than 16 milliseconds, your users experience jank" (cookbook).
  • Graph memory (our arithmetic from Dart VM object headers UNVERIFIED, to be measured in DevTools): about 20k nodes and 100k directed edges take roughly 5–7 MB as Dart objects, against about 1.1 MB as CSR typed arrays (Float32List/Int32List).
    • Typed arrays hold no pointers for the garbage collector to trace.
    • Isolate.exit transfers results "without copying" (Dart).
  • SQLite primary docs:
    • Small blobs are read and written "35% faster" than individual files, using "about 20% less disk space". The Android figure came from a Galaxy S3 and is called "approximate" (fasterthanfs).
    • immutable=1 signals that the file "cannot be modified" (URI docs). That is safe here because a published version never changes (Frozen Package Rule).
    • FTS5 gives prefix queries and bm25() ranking (FTS5).
    • R*Tree is "designed for doing range queries… most commonly used in geospatial systems" (R*Tree).
  • Precedent for SQLite as a map container:
    • MBTiles: "Tilesets SHALL be valid SQLite databases" (spec).
    • OGC GeoPackage: "a platform-independent SQLite database file", "particularly useful on mobile devices… with limited connectivity" (spec).
    • Counter-precedent: OsmAnd (.obf) and Organic Maps (.mwm) use custom binary formats, and Mappedin ships a zip of GeoJSON (MVF).
  • Server-side writer: better-sqlite3 13.0.3 (2026-08-05) uses N-API prebuilds, with Node 26 support, and bundles SQLite 3.53.4 with FTS5 and R*Tree. Built-in node:sqlite is still "Stability: 1.2 - Release candidate" in the Node 24 and 26 docs (Node).
  • Rejected stores:
    • Hive CE, Isar, isar_community, ObjectBox and sembast all describe themselves as NoSQL or object stores, which is a rule-fit risk.
    • Isar is abandoned ("Isar is abandoned", #1689, 2025-01-07).
    • Their published benchmarks are run by the vendors themselves.
  • No Android-device timing exists for the chosen setup UNVERIFIED. Spike 2 measures it.
Option (0.4 shortlist)StrengthsWeaknesses
Prebuilt read-only SQLite + CSR graphNo whole-file parse. Indexed FTS5 search and R*Tree hit-testing are built in. Graph about 1.1 MB. One worker isolate owns the file and runs A*. Evict = delete a fileTwo libraries the mandate doesn't name. A SQLite dialect beside T-SQL. Device timings unmeasured
FlatBuffers binary + own index"Access the data directly without unpacking or parsing" (flatbuffers.dev, vendor). Smallest memory footprint. No databaseSearch, ranking and polygon hit-testing all hand-built. flat_buffers comes from an unverified uploader
Plain JSON + index (0.3 pick)Simplest. Clean under any reading of the ruleSlowest cold start, and the most RAM on 2–4 GB phones
sqflite + raw SQLClean under any reading of the ruleNo FTS5 or R*Tree on stock Android. Method-channel serialization on every call
Ruling · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.4

A prebuilt read-only SQLite package per venue version, with the routing graph as a CSR blob, built on the server with better-sqlite3. This differs from the 0.3 recommendation (plain files) and was chosen on performance.

  • Server: the publish engine writes one SQLite file per published version. It holds only allow-listed tables and no secrets (Sealed Package Rule). It is gzipped for download and never rewritten (Frozen Package Rule).
  • Device: sqlite3 3.x opens each package with ?mode=ro&immutable=1, inside one long-lived worker isolate that also runs A*. The graph blob loads into Float32List/Int32List, and rows never cross isolates.
  • Mutable state: the package registry and telemetry queue live in a separate small database in WAL mode, with one writer.
  • All SQL is hand-written and parameterized. Conditional exception E-01 is withdrawn, since the drift typed API was not chosen.
  • Still owed to Allan (or Raphael) before the P0 gate: a note on the two libraries the mandate doesn't name, better-sqlite3 (backend) and sqlite3 (device), plus H5 (the scope of the no-ORM rule). L115: signed by the product owner, under delegation from Raphael Mendoza (held since the project started): E-02 and E-03 approved; H5 ruled, the no-ORM rule covers the phone too.
  • Spike 2 benchmarks this format against FlatBuffers on a low-end phone before the schema locks.

D7Phase 0 spikes

Ruled 2026-09-25

These are throwaway spikes in a separate scratch repo. They produce numbers, not code to keep.

  • Spike 1, Android field devices: range 3–5 iBeacon-format beacons on a Transsion, a Xiaomi and a Samsung. Record detection rate, RSSI stability, and behaviour under the scan throttle and with the screen off.
    • Also confirm the same code builds for iOS, and record which command produces a full compile.
    • Body-shadowing bench: the phone held in front, in a pocket, and behind the body, with one to three people standing 1–3 m away in the line of sight. Record the RSSI change and whether the nearest-beacon choice flips.
      • For reference, the literature measures about 13–15 dB extra loss when the user's own head blocks the path (Etzlinger et al. 2021), and about 5 dB for one person 1–3 m away (PMC6960901).
      • This is a bench limit, not a crowd measurement. The crowd measurement waits for a pilot venue (M6).
    • One phone must be a model with no gyroscope (Tecno Spark Go or Redmi A3 class).
    • Spike beacons don't commit fleet procurement (D9).
    • Added for D4b 0.5, on each phone:
      • the isOffloadedFilteringSupported() and batching flags;
      • the sensor list, including whether TYPE_ROTATION_VECTOR exists without a gyroscope;
      • per-model RSSI offsets against a reference phone;
      • the callback rate under SCAN_MODE_LOW_LATENCY with the single ScanFilter;
      • behaviour at the 10-min scan timeout on Android 14+;
      • magnetometer magnitude near escalators, to tune the #5 gate;
      • "select where you are" picker latency on the FTS5 package.
  • Spike 2, routing and rendering: a synthetic 4-floor graph of about 20k nodes. Time A* in a Dart isolate on a low-end Android phone, render with flutter_map CrsSimple, and measure the gzipped package size.
Recommendation (draft 0.3, wording fixed in 0.4)

Run both. Effort: S. It turns the plan's weakest numbers into measurements before the schema is locked. Needs an owner's OK. (0.4: the wording "a few days" was replaced by "Effort: S", closing C10.)

Ruling · product owner · 2026-09-25 New in 0.4

Run both, with these additions:

  • Spike 1 adds a realme phone to the Transsion, Xiaomi and Samsung. (0.9: all four are on hand; the Samsung is the RSSI reference phone.) realme phones generally lack a barometer (epey), so this phone also tests floor detection without one.
  • Spike 1 includes a QR-scan test on each phone. That is half of C12a's fix; the other half, tagging the claim in D4, waits for the D4 pass.
  • Spike 1's numbers feed the deferred D4 discussion.
  • Spike 2 also benchmarks the D6 format. It measures open time, FTS5 search and the graph blob load against a FlatBuffers build, on a low-end phone.
Spike scope, consolidated · rulings L46–L49 · 2026-09-28 New in 0.9

What it is: a throwaway Flutter app in the scratch repo FlutterProjects\gabay_spike. It produces numbers, not code: nothing is ported into Gabay. Raphael approved the spikes (L49).

  • Spike 1c: live dot on the penthouse (L47).
    • The penthouse map: the derived outline, with sheet ID-01 as a faint underlay (L48).
    • B1–B5, the doors and 20 tape-measured floor marks.
    • Three live dots, each with an uncertainty halo: (a) nearest beacon; (b) weighted centroid; (c) a grid HMM that uses steps and RSSI with wall and door constraints (D4b-lite).
    • An "I'm here" button logs each method's error at a mark. Per run it reports time to first fix, median and p95 error, room-correct rate and battery.
    • This closes a gap: D4d and L36 need a measured dot error, and Spike 1 as written computed no position.
    • 0.11 Door-level goal (L53, L55): the penthouse is scored as a mall floor. "I'm here" at a door mark also logs whether the grid HMM's nearest destination door is the right one, and the Runs tab shows the "right door" rate. The spike has no routing (Spike 2 only benchmarks it), so arrival at a door is recorded for when routing is built.
  • Build order:
    • Phase A: discover and assign the H2s, the map and live dot, and runs.
    • Phase B: the Spike 1 measurement screens (device info, calibration against the Samsung, body shadowing, the 10-minute timeout, magnetometer, QR).
    • Phase C: the Spike 2 benchmarks.
  • Tester tools 0.10:
    • an in-app beacon position editor (L50). Moves are stored on the phone as overrides; the file's format is unchanged. They can be exported as venue JSON, and each move is logged in the run CSV;
    • Simple / Technical wording (L51), with Simple as the default, plus a "How to test" guide for non-technical testers.
    • 0.12 a mall view with labelled store blocks, a walkway and icons, and a store name, category and colour editor saved as on-phone overrides (L57).
    • 0.13 furniture drawn as blocked, walkable cells shaded, and a draggable walkway network (L58, L59).
  • BLE and sensors: Gabay's own thin Kotlin channel (L23). BLUETOOTH_SCAN is declared without neverForLocation, because that flag "filters out some BLE beacons" (Android).
  • Out of scope:
    • the backend, accounts, the product's map editor, publishing and analytics (0.10: the spike's own beacon position editor, L50, is a test tool, not the product editor; 0.21: L70 and L71 add an on-phone admin side for internal testers only, which is not the product editor, publishing or analytics; 0.25: L75 adds an on-phone venue editor for testers only, which is not the product's map editor);
    • the mall site and outdoor positioning;
    • production-quality code;
    • the MKGW3 gateway, parked (L46);
    • the iOS build check, deferred to P1 CI because there is no Mac (L49).
  • What the outputs feed:
    • D4d, from the dot error and first fix;
    • D8, from coverage and density;
    • D9, from the plausibility tuning;
    • Blueprint 4.3's parameters;
    • the D6 format (Spike 2);
    • the L36 pass or fail values.

D8Beacon economics

New in 0.3Held until after the app is built (L114)

Who pays for, owns, installs and maintains beacons, and does positioning stage 2 ship at all?

Terminology note (0.6) New in 0.6: this held decision keeps its 0.3 wording. In current terms, "stage 1" = the location fallback (D4a) and "stage 2" = the live position engine (D4b). D8 now waits on the prototype's results and D4d (L24).

Evidence

  • Density guidance varies about 10x: 1 per 300–400 m² (Situm, vendor); 1 per 74–149 m² (Pointr, vendor); every 5–15 m in MLoc's peer-reviewed deployment.
  • Hardware and upkeep: $5–35 a unit (vendor pages). Battery life 24–75 months at −12 dBm and 350 ms (Kontakt spec). Installation takes 4–8 weeks per 100,000 m² (Pointr, vendor; UNVERIFIED).
  • Failures: in MLoc, beacon failures reached 4.9% after a year, many falling off ceilings. Retail beacon rollouts stalled partly over battery upkeep (Total Retail).
  • Scale: 250–1,350 beacons and roughly $2k–47k of hardware per 100,000 m², before installation, calibration and upkeep (our arithmetic).
  • Gabay's position: one developer, no pilot, and a competitor already piloting at SM North EDSA. The availability and price of sentinel hardware (fixed BLE scanners) is UNVERIFIED, and is researched as part of this decision.
OptionStrengthsWeaknesses
A. The mall buys and owns the hardware, to Gabay's specGabay holds no inventory or capital. Gabay sells the placement plan, configuration and fleet monitoringA slower sale, since the mall needs capital approval. Installation quality is outside Gabay's control
B. Gabay supplies hardware as a serviceOne accountable vendor. Recurring revenueInventory, capital and a field operation for a solo developer with no pilot. Highest risk
C. Stage 1 only until a paying venue commitsZero hardware risk. The product works on stage 1No blue dot to demo against a competitor that may have one
D. Partner with a beacon vendorProven hardware and logisticsVendor rotating-ID schemes resolve in the vendor's cloud, which conflicts with invariant 4. Lock-in
Recommendation

A, gated by C.

  • Stage 1 is the baseline product for every venue.
  • Stage 2 is a per-venue option on hardware the mall owns, bought to Gabay's spec (D9), with sentinels included. Its software is built when the first venue commits.
  • If chosen, stage 2 becomes Medium → P2 and moves to milestone M7. Under B it stays High → P0.
  • Rule on D8 before the P0 gate. L114 lifts this: the product owner holds D8 until after the app is built.

D9Beacon identity scheme

New in 0.3Ruled 2026-10-07 (L114)

What identity do Gabay beacons broadcast, given that iBeacon identities can be cloned? This is a security question, the top of the audit's tiebreak order.

Evidence

  • iBeacon has no authentication. It carries "UUID 16 bytes, Major 2 bytes, Minor 2 bytes", and the UUID can be "sniffed off the air" (Apple, 2014).
  • Cloning: "BLE advertising transmissions used by beacons are unprotected, thus making cloning easy". A signature alone "would not work … It has to combine with a changing ID" (Chan & Chung).
  • Demonstrated harm: impersonating iBeacons with ordinary Wi-Fi hardware caused errors "more than 20 meters" (Wi-attack, SECON 2021).
  • Eddystone-EID needs a resolver that holds the key (spec). Nearby Messages stopped in Dec 2023 (Google). The Proximity Beacon API shutdown date is UNVERIFIED.
  • Vendor schemes (Kontakt Secure Shuffling, Estimote Secure UUID; partially verified) resolve IDs in the vendor's cloud. That sends observed IDs off the device, which breaks invariant 4.
  • Relay: rotating IDs don't stop it: "cannot be spoofed, except by a real-time relay" (Google, 2016).
  • Room for a signature: legacy advertising is capped at 31 octets (Bluetooth SIG), leaving about 1 byte beside iBeacon's fields UNVERIFIED.
  • Map-based plausibility checks are published only as server-side systems (85% detection, 5% false alarms, IWQoS 2016). Running them on the device is Gabay's adaptation, and how well that works is UNVERIFIED.
OptionStrengthsWeaknesses
A. Static iBeacon in a Gabay namespace, plus plausibility and anchor cross-checks on the deviceCommodity beacons. Works through iOS CoreLocation. No keys. Invariant 4 intactA well-placed clone goes undetected. On-device detection is UNVERIFIED
B. Vendor rotating IDs, resolved in the cloudStops casual cloningBreaks invariant 4. Needs connectivity. Lock-in. Doesn't stop relay
C. Rotating IDs resolved on the device, with keys in the packageWorks offlineRejected: the package is public, so the keys would be too (Sealed Package Rule)
D. A custom signed advertisement, verified on the device with a public keyOffline. Only a public key goes in the packageCustom firmware. Needs extended advertising or a custom frame, and CoreBluetooth on iOS. Relay still works. Highest build cost

When this has to be decided. The costly thing to change after the P0 gate is the hardware already bought, not the schema: migrations only add. So the cheap parts go in before the P0 gate:

  • the Gabay namespace
  • the IdentityScheme column
  • the Sealed Package Rule
  • the threat model

D9 itself must be settled before any fleet purchase. L114 (2026-10-07): ruled option A, static iBeacon in a Gabay namespace with a range per floor and checks on the phone.

Input noted (L96). The BLE beacon count calculator's rule: give each floor its own identifier range, so the app never trusts a signal bleeding through the slab. Whichever option D9 picks should reserve a range per level. Per-floor beacon grouping is already part of D4b.

Recommendation

A now, with the schema ready for D. B is off the table unless the product owner and the DPO first amend invariant 4.

Prototype: two tracks Updated in 0.7

Ruling L34 · product owner · 2026-09-28 New in 0.7

The prototype becomes the product; this supersedes L21. Standard Appendix C.1 Phase 0 says "Not in: any code", so the work runs on two tracks:

  • Track 1: measurement spikes (throwaway, run now). The D7 Spike 1 and Spike 2, in a scratch repo, before the P0 gate. Their numbers settle D4d, then D8 and D9, which breaks the D4d ↔ P0-gate loop. They need Allan's or Raphael's OK, and no code from them is kept.
  • Track 2: the product prototype (the real repo). Order: P0 documents and the schema review → P0 gate → P1 rails → P2 identity and tenancy → P4, shopper side first. It must demonstrate:
    • the live dot on budget phones;
    • routing and package speed;
    • the end-to-end shopper flow;
    • a minimal admin side;
    • all on the full mandated stack (Flutter and riverpod; Node, Express, MSSQL and JWT).

Test site (L35): first a small real space (100–300 m², 5–10 beacons: Faragher & Harle 2015 measured <2.6 m at 1 per 30 m² and <4.8 m at 1 per 100 m²). Then, with permission, a mall corridor with an escalator, for body-shadowing, crowd, floor-change and steel-disturbance data. 0.8 (L43): the small real space is the Dynamiq penthouse (about 123 m², 5 H2 beacons).

Success metrics (L36). Pass thresholds, measured per phone model and off-peak. Every target is UNVERIFIED until measured, and every result is recorded, pass or fail.

Prototype floor: Dynamiq penthouse New in 0.8

Its 5 MOKOSmart H2 beacons go in layout A below, and a 6th is the next purchase if the D7 walk shows gaps (L44). 0.11 (L53–L55) New in 0.11: the spike's goal is now door-level guidance: the penthouse is treated as a mall floor, and a trip ends on reaching the destination's entrance (L53). A "concourse ring" layout is proposed to replace layout A below. Layout A stays in force until the product owner approves the ring on its simulation results (L54). Success is scored at the doors with the "arrived at the right door" rate (L55).

0.13 (L58–L60) New in 0.13: the floor model is corrected to ID-01. Clothing and Dunkin are pass-through stores. Furniture is modelled as walking obstacles at 1 dB, and a walkway network with wheelchair flags is added. The beacons stand on tables at 0.75 m instead of the ceiling.

Source drawings: Modernbrands sheets ID-01 (furniture layout), ID-02 (reflected ceiling plan) and ID-03 (power & auxiliary), dated 01/13/23, dimensions in mm. The sheets are marked "PROPERTY OF MODERNBRANDS INC. DO NOT COPY". 0.9 (L48): Raphael Mendoza gave permission to use them, internally only. The spike app may show ID-01 as an underlay, but the drawings stay in internal repos and out of public or shared artifacts.

Floor model (derived)

Coordinates are metres from the north-west outer corner: x runs east along the 13,725 mm dimension, and y runs south along the 11,070 mm dimension. Room edges are read from the dimension chains and the drawing scale, to about ±0.3 m UNVERIFIED. Tape-check them on site before the demo venue seed is written.

ItemValueFrom
Extent13.725 × 11.07 m; about 123 m² gross, about 100 m² walkableID-01 dimension chains

  • Mini Conference;
  • Office 1 (the open hub behind the main entrance);
  • Office 2 and Office 3;
  • Mini Pantry;

  • the server racks;
  • ceiling CCTV.
ID-02, ID-03

Method

A grid simulation over the derived model, used only to rank layouts against each other. It does not predict accuracy, and every input is UNVERIFIED until the D7 walk survey.

  • Grid and heights: 0.25 m cells over the walkable area; beacon on the ceiling at 2.4 m, phone at 1.1 m.
  • Signal model: log-distance with exponent n = 3, from ITU-R P.1238's office distance power-loss coefficient N = 30 (ITU).
  • Wall loss: 3 dB per partition. NIST measured about 0.6 dB for ½″ drywall and about 1.4 dB for ¼″ glass at 2 GHz (NISTIR 6055, via a search snippet), so 3 dB is conservative.
  • Columns and shafts: 20 dB each. Reinforced concrete runs 15–25 dB (secondary source).
  • Body margin: 5 dB (L40 step 4).
  • Candidates and search: mounts every 0.5 m, none in the CRs or the server area, at least 3 m apart; local search with 16 restarts.
  • Objective: half the share of cells that hear three or more beacons, half the share where the strongest beacon is in the phone's own room. A first run that optimised coverage alone bunched beacons in the hall and lost room discrimination, so that objective was dropped.
  • Sensitivity: the default 0 dBm transmit power; a stricter usable level of −85 dBm; 6 dB per wall.

Results (base case: +4 dBm, usable ≥ −90 dBm, 3 dB per wall)

LayoutBeaconsHear ≥ 1Hear ≥ 3HDOP p95Strongest in own room
A: optimised (recommended)5100%71% (Office 1: 99%)1.7677%
B: one per room centre5100%62%2.0378%
K: corners5100%24%2.1255%
C6: A + hall (4.0, 4.6)6100%86%2.1981%
C7: C6 + Office 2 south (4.0, 10.5)7100%90%1.8681%

At 3 to 9 beacons, the unconstrained optimiser gains 18 points of three-beacon coverage for the 6th beacon, 6 for the 7th and 1 for the 8th.

Robustness:

  • A matches or beats B, and beats K, on three-beacon coverage and on geometry in all four scenarios. B scores about 1 point higher on "strongest in own room" in three of them.
  • Every layout keeps "hear ≥ 1" at 100% in every scenario.
  • A's three-beacon coverage falls to 45% at 0 dBm and to 43% at 6 dB per wall.
  • The cells that hear fewer than three beacons are in dead-end rooms: the CRs, the far side of the conference room and the south desks of Office 2. There, knowing the room is enough.

Layout A: where the 5 H2s go

It suits D4b (BLE + steps + walkway-graph matching). The hub, Office 1, gets a three-beacon fix, and the end rooms need only room-level certainty.

#x, y (m)Mount (ceiling)Covers

Settings for the D7 spike:

  • Transmit power: +4 dBm, the H2's maximum ("+4 dBm maximum and down to -40dBm", MOKOBlue).
  • Frame: iBeacon (see D4); UUID from the namespace (invariant 9); Major and Minor per venue and beacon.
  • RSSI@1m: calibrated on site, using the H2's configurable measured-power field.
  • Advertising interval: tested at 100, 250 and 1000 ms. The brief's "up to 5 years" battery life is for 1 slot, 1000 ms and 0 dBm, so the cost of +4 dBm and a faster interval is UNVERIFIED and gets measured.

Mounting rules. DESIGN CHOICE; the alternative is walls at 2.0 m.

  • Flat on the ceiling, using the H2's double-sided sticker. Kontakt.io puts ceiling beacons at 2.5–3.25 m (snippet), and Estimote says ceiling mounting "also works" (snippet).
  • At least 1 m from Wi-Fi mesh points and ACUs. This is a precaution without evidence: BLE advertising channels 37/38/39 sit between Wi-Fi channels 1, 6 and 11 (SemFio).
  • Nothing inside the server area, and never on the concrete columns.

How many beacons for the most efficiency:

  • 5 (owned) is enough for the prototype. It is 1 per 25 m² gross, denser than the 1 per 30 m² at which Faragher & Harle measured "<2.6 m error 95% of the time" (2015). That was fingerprinting, not D4b.
  • 6 is the knee: +15 points of three-beacon coverage in the base case, +16 at 0 dBm and +18 at 6 dB per wall. Buy one more, for C6 in the hall, if the walk shows gaps there or in the conference room. C6 is hand-placed, 2.4 m from B1; the 3 m minimum applied only to the optimiser.
  • 7 only if D4d chooses pure trilateration.
  • Beyond 7, each beacon adds under 2 points.

Alternatives:

  • B (room centres): as good for proximity-only positioning, and easier to explain to installers. It is weaker for multilateration.
  • K (corners): rejected. Corner guidance assumes a single open room, and here the partitions block the lines between corners.

Routing, editor and BLE planner design New in 0.7

Ruling L37 · routing default

The router always recommends the single shortest route, measured by fastest travel time. Shortest walking distance, "distance with elevator time", and the second- and third-shortest routes are shown only when the shopper asks for alternatives. Step-free mode applies the same rule without stairs or escalators.

Connector cost model (seconds):

ElementCostEvidence
Walkwayd ÷ 1.2 m/sDefault walking speed UNVERIFIED; tuned in the spikes
EscalatorL = rise ÷ sin 30°; time = L ÷ 0.5 m/s + 3 sASME A17.1 §6.1.3.1: "not to exceed 30 deg" (UpCodes). 35° only if the rise is ≤ 6 m (EN 115, snippet). 0.5 m/s is from vendor snippets UNVERIFIED
Stairssteps = ⌈rise ÷ 0.20⌉; slope = √(rise² + (steps × 0.25)²); time = slope ÷ 0.6 m/s + 2 s per landingPD 1096 §1207(e): rise "shall not exceed 200 millimeters", run "not less than 250 millimeters" (PD 1096). Stair speed UNVERIFIED
Elevator (L38, per car)W + stops × 8 s + rise ÷ v + 2 s; v = 1.0–1.6 m/s; W defaults to 30 s and the admin can set itADA §407.3.5: doors open 3 s minimum (UpCodes). The 8 s door cycle and the speeds are consultant and vendor figures; a retail wait figure UNVERIFIED. MuNES (2024) costs elevator waiting time inside A* (arXiv)
Rampd ÷ walking speed, at a 1:20 gradient by defaultBP 344 revised IRR 2024: "Gradient not steeper than 1:20" (NCDA). This corrects the 1:12 assumption
Ruling L39 · floor editor (subject to change)

Four approaches, chosen per the admin's preference:

  1. Grid boxes plus an object library (P0): game-style placement with real dimensions, for example a 40×40 m floor, a 9×9 m store, a 1×1 m pillar.
  2. Polygon tool (P0): curved atriums, angled walls and the outer boundary.
  3. Traced floor-plan underlay (P0): upload an image or PDF, calibrate it with two points and a known distance, and trace over it (§3.15 uploads).
  4. CAD/IMDF import (P1).
  • Every shape can be resized, moved and rotated.
  • The floor size and grid cell size are set in real metres.
  • Stairs and escalators carry a height; elevators carry the L38 parameters.
  • Walkable and passable areas are derived automatically (the floor minus its boxes and polygons), and the walkway graph is auto-generated at publish (L32).
  • Admins build floor by floor.

Grid note: 0.5 m cells give 6,400 nodes on a 40×40 m floor, 1 m gives 1,600, and 2 m gives 400 (arithmetic). Coarse cells can close off narrow gaps, so the editor grid is 0.5–1 m and routing uses a reduced walkway graph that stays within the Spike 2 budget UNVERIFIED.

Ruling L40 · BLE placement planner (subject to change)

From each floor model, the planner proposes three plans:

  • minimum proximity;
  • recommended tracking;
  • budget-capped.

Each plan gives the beacon count, a cost estimate (with prices entered by the admin), and coverage and GDOP heat-maps. The admin picks one.

  1. Inputs: the floor model, cell size, boxes and polygons, connectors with heights, entrances and ceiling height.
  2. Walkable grid → walkway centreline graph. The same graph is used for map-matching.
  3. Candidate mounts on walls and pillar faces at 3–4 m ("ideally 3 to 4 meters", Esri, vendor). Avoid "large metal infrastructure" and elevator shafts. 0.8 (L45): mount at the highest point available; when the ceiling is below 3 m, mount on the ceiling (Kontakt.io: 2.5–3.25 m, snippet). The penthouse ceiling is 2.25–2.6 m.
  4. RSSI model: log-distance, plus penalties per wall or pillar, plus a body margin of about 5 dB (Sensors 2021).
  5. Mandatory beacons first: 1–2 per connector landing for floor detection (D4b #8), and an inside/outside pair at each entrance (L31).
  6. Greedy k-coverage: k = 3 on corridors and k = 4 in open atriums, using submodular set cover ("6 ~ 23%… fewer beacons", Wang 2019, snippet). Stagger in a zig-zag at 10–15 m (Esri).
  7. Geometry refinement with simulated annealing or a genetic algorithm, minimising the 95th-percentile GDOP and rejecting collinear triples. GDOP-aware placement cut beacons by "22% and 60% (33% on an average)" (Rajagopal 2016). Optimised layouts reached "GDOP value between 2 to 5", against about 100 for random placement (OPTILOD).
  8. Density check: flag any zone sparser than 1 per 100 m², and offer an option near 1 per 30 m² (Faragher & Harle 2015).
  9. Outputs: positions, mount heights, count, cost (units × price + install + batteries per year), and heat-maps.

    • Google Play: US$25 once (Play). An organisation account needs a D-U-N-S number, which "can take up to 30 days" (Play). A new personal account must "run a closed test with at least 12 opted-in testers continuously for 14 days" (Play). Decide the account type now
    • a DPO and data-processing agreements
    • owner review time
    • a macOS CI runner ($0.062/min against $0.006 on Linux, private repos; GitHub)
    • no pilot client yet
  • GABAY_MASTER_BLUEPRINT.md Parts 1–3, plus the Part 4 section anchors (4.1 publish engine, 4.2 routing engine, 4.3 positioning, 4.4 package delivery & shopper shell, 4.5 venue boundary guard, 4.6 map editing, 4.7 directory & search, 4.8 shopper accounts, 4.9 live venue status, 4.10 BLE placement planner; 4.6–4.8 and the 4.4 rename added in 0.6, 4.9–4.10 in 0.7). The anchors exist so pipeline entries can cite Blueprint §4.x (H7).

P1

Rails & guardrails

  • Founding Build step, first thing after the P0 gate: Blueprint Parts 4–6 and PRD §9, in one commit, with PRD §9 and Blueprint Part 5 identical row for row. Part 6 opens with the named rules.
  • Layout:
    • backend/
    • db/: zero-padded migrations, seeds
    • one Flutter project with main_mobile.dart and main_admin.dart
    Device code sits behind interfaces in a platform-neutral core/. The native BLE channel (Kotlin and Swift) sits under core/positioning/ (L23, 0.6).
  • CI builds Android, iOS without code signing, and Web on every PR. Flutter documents flutter build ios --release --no-codesign --config-only (Flutter); a full unsigned build is UNVERIFIED and is checked in Spike 1.
  • Backend:
    • §3.1 bootstrap, §3.8 error shape, §3.9 DB layer plus the GO splitter, §3.10 dates, §3.11 pagination, §3.18 actor context
    • §3.16: .env.example, fail-fast, and the GlobalSettings table and resolvers
    • GET /api/health
    • the pino logger
  • Frontend:
    • §4.1, §4.3, §4.7
    • §4.2 per D5 (riverpod): one ProviderScope, with Appendix A.12's canonical code adapted to it once
    • the §4.6 shared/ skeleton (components may be stubs)
    • AppVersion with two version streams (mobile and web), and ChangelogEntry per surface
  • Process:
    • git hooks and the README line that enables them
    • CI
    • one npm run verify that works on Windows
    • REVIEW.md, Claude hooks and a verifier agent
    • a one-page CLAUDE.md
    • §8.2: a re-runnable schema.sql, idempotent migrations that run a verification query, and the setup-db orchestrator
    • §8.5: the companion-file sync table, which includes the routing test-vector file and the package-format version
  • End-to-end harness (§7.1): a phase function per module, all awaited from main(). Deterministic seeds (a demo tenant and a simulated venue). The first phase is health.
  • Drift linter, blocking from P1: in test:syntax, in pre-commit, and as a hook after every Flutter edit. Warn mode is the retrofit device (C.2 R2), and C.1's own P1 gate requires a bare TextFormField to be blocked.
  • Catastrophic-mistake linters:
    • tenant and venue predicate
    • published-version immutability
    • position privacy: from 0.7 (L42), position may leave the device only through the consented analytics module (lib/core/analytics/), and only after an opt-in consent check
    • Public-Read, including the package-field allow-list (Sealed Package Rule)
  • Foreground manifest lint 0.6, in test:syntax. It enforces the Foreground Rule and D4a (no background detection), and closes C8a. It fails on any of:
    • ACCESS_BACKGROUND_LOCATION in an Android manifest;
    • a location foreground-service type;
    • iOS location in UIBackgroundModes;
    • an NSLocationAlwaysAndWhenInUseUsageDescription string.
  • §12 items that land here: 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 11, 12 (see the §12 walk).
  • Weekly end-of-life job (§1 [P1]):
    • Node and SQL Server: read from endoflife.date (nodejs, mssqlserver). The job fails within 90 days of end of life.
    • Flutter and Dart: not on endoflife.date. Only the current stable gets security fixes, so the job fails when the pin isn't the current stable.
    • Packages: reported as "outdated", never "EOL", because none of them publish an end-of-support date.
    The exact API path is UNVERIFIED. It is accepted as a risk until the job's first run, and that run's result is recorded in the pin table (0.6, closes C12b).

GateA deliberately bad commit is blocked on a repo with zero features. The test cases:

  • a colour literal
  • interpolated SQL
  • a bare TextFormField
  • an UPDATE to a published version
  • a CREATE TABLE without its DROP TABLE
  • an edit to a committed migration
  • a POST route on the public router
  • a package field outside the allow-list
  • a background-location permission in a manifest (0.6)

Also: Blueprint Parts 4–6 and PRD §9 exist, and the milestone-mirror lint confirms PRD §9 and Part 5 match. GET /api/health answers, and the end-to-end harness's health phase exits 0.

P2

Identity, access, tenancy, audit

  • Admin auth (§3.3). Roles, per the L41 matrix (0.7): SuperAdmin (IQ), IQ/Mall Admin, Venue Editor and Viewer/Auditor in the MVP, with access granted per venue. Merchant, Units and Maintenance come in the next release. (Before 0.7: platform admin, operator admin, venue editor, viewer.) The §3.4 three authorization layers, §3.5 tenant context, the §3.6 audit trail and the §3.2 rate limits.
  • The GlobalSettings admin screen: the lockout threshold and the single-session toggle (§12 item 10).
  • A venue switcher, with a tenant header override honoured for superusers only.
  • zod at every route boundary, including the public API (§12 item 1).
  • The first maintenance screens (users, roles, venue grants), built on a minimal real FieldSpec resolver and list scaffold. P3 completes them (H2).
  • The public shopper API: unauthenticated, read-only, published versions only, cacheable, and rate-limited with carrier-grade NAT in mind.
  • Shopper identity (D1) New in 0.4: its own tables, separate from admin identity, built on the same §3.3 machinery. Shopper sign-in and deletion routes live outside backend/routes/public/, which stays GET-only. The shopper-facing feature ships in P4.
  • End-to-end phases for auth, RBAC and tenant isolation. Manual rows grow from here onward.

GateThe §6 security gate passes on a system that does nothing yet, including the public API's read-only and published-only guarantees.

  • Version badge and what's-new dialog on both surfaces (§4.8 [P1]; §5 item 9).
  • The list scaffold completed, with the P2 user list moved onto it (§5 item 11).
  • The editor is a canvas. The drift linter covers its property panels, not its geometry.
  • Negative space: no skills plugin and no evals/, per the standard's own carve-out ("The eval suite (§7.2) is carved out of the next project"). If that is lifted, the plugin and evals/ arrive together (H6).

GateEach of §5's twelve items has its verdict recorded in the §5 table, checked item by item. The drift linter is green, blocking, wired into pre-commit, and has caught at least one real mismatch; if it has never fired, a deliberately staged mismatch proves it runs.

Why check beacon geometry now. Where beacons go when the live position engine is installed (formerly "stage 2") is what limits the engine's accuracy later, and re-mounting hundreds of beacons is the expensive step.

  • Error grows as the angles between beacons shrink (Famili et al. 2022).
  • Beacons in a straight line make the error unbounded. That is our own derivation from the paper's formula.
  • The provisional threshold is the paper's "bad" band (above 20).
  • Zig-zag placement in corridors is vendor guidance (Esri).

Build order (shopper first, L34) Updated 0.7

Venue, building & level→ Demo venue seed→ Publish engine (auto graph)→ Shopper shell · Routing engine→ Directory · Step-free · Location fallback · Shopper accounts→ Venue boundary→ Live position engine (tier and milestone per D4d · D8)→ Map editor · BLE placement planner

Before 0.7 the order ran venue and level → map editor → publish engine → … . The demo venue seed now lets the shopper side come first, and the full editor follows.

P5

Release gate

  • Standard checks:
    • §6 again
    • the smoke guide, completed and revised in place
    • the per-release slice guide
    • §4.8 re-verified
    • the runbook, and the first per-release deploy plan
    • a restore drill
    • each §12 item verified individually
    • then the audit prompt
  • Privacy obligations (Data Privacy Act):
    • a data-processing agreement (the mall is probably the controller, Gabay the processor)
    • a DPO
    • a privacy notice, plus a child-oriented version (NPC Advisory 2024-03), both covering shopper account data (D1)
    • account deletion verified in the app and on the web, as Apple and Google Play require (D1)
    • just-in-time location consent (Circular 2023-04)
    • a 72-hour breach runbook (Circular 16-03)
    • an NPC registration check (Circular 2022-04)
  • No pilot yet: run the gate against a fully mapped simulated venue.
  • Staged store release: Google Play internal testing, then closed testing on the Android field devices. Then iOS: developer account, an iPhone test pass, TestFlight, the App Store.
  • Admin web deployment: hosting, TLS, API_BASE, and a supported-browser statement.
  • Store listings and in-app text make no numeric accuracy claim beyond PRD §7.

GateAn audit report with zero Critical and zero High findings. Every applicable §12 item is marked verified, with evidence. The reviewer confirms that store listings and in-app copy make no numeric accuracy claim beyond PRD §7. Medium and Low findings become pipeline entries.

P6

Cadence

Quarterly:

  • an audit diff
  • an EXCEPTIONS.md expiry sweep (E-01 was withdrawn in 0.4; the sweep covers any later entries)
  • a review of the reports from the weekly end-of-life job (the job is the enforcer; the review is the human check on it)

Beacon battery lifecycle is part of operations. bands.yaml metrics:

  • post-deploy 5xx rate
  • package-download failure rate
  • "no route found" rate
  • audit-table growth

Plus these three, all from sentinel data, with no shopper data:

- metric: beacon_duplicate_sighting     # G1: one identity heard by two sentinels farther apart than its range
  baseline: rolling_30d
  rules: western_electric
  tiers: { 1sigma: {action: log}, 2sigma: {action: diagnose}, 3sigma: {action: propose, routes: [pull_request]} }
- metric: reference_rssi_depression     # G2: hourly median RSSI per sentinel-beacon pair vs the same hour-of-week
  baseline: rolling_28d_same_hour
  rules: western_electric
  tiers: { 1sigma: {action: log}, 2sigma: {action: diagnose} }
- metric: beacon_silent_count           # sentinel-sourced; MLoc saw failures reach 4.9% in year one
  baseline: rolling_30d
  rules: western_electric
  tiers: { 1sigma: {action: log}, 2sigma: {action: diagnose}, 3sigma: {action: propose, routes: [pull_request]} }

GateNone. This phase recurs.

Blueprint additions

Part 1: cross-cutting invariants

  1. Naming: "tenant" means the mall-operator company. Stores are Occupants.
  2. Coordinates: local planar metres per level, plus a georeference transform per venue.
  3. Published venue versions are immutable.
  4. Shopper position never leaves the device, except through the opt-in route-analytics module: consent is off by default, sought through a just-in-time prompt, unbundled from other consents and withdrawable as easily as it was given, and traces are pseudonymised. Amended in 0.7 (L42) Legal gate: DPO, counsel and the NPC registration check before implementation (0.3–0.6 wording: "unless aggregated above a minimum-count threshold".)
  5. Navigation is a foreground session.
  6. One routing algorithm, shared as test vectors.
  7. Beacon observations are evidence, not identity. No single observation places the shopper. 0.3
  8. No secret material in any venue package. 0.3
  9. Gabay assigns each tenant one beacon UUID. (UUID, major, minor) is unique platform-wide, enforced by the database. 0.3
  10. Every position is either inside the venue boundary or declared outside it (the IMDF Venue polygon: "ALL other features MUST be within the polygonal boundary of the Venue", OGC IMDF). 0.3

Part 3: data model

  • IMDF vocabulary as tables: Venue, Level, Unit, Opening, Amenity, Occupant and Anchor, all carrying TenantId.
  • Routing graph: Gabay's own NavNode, NavEdge and NavConnector, because IMDF excludes routing graphs.
  • Versions: VenueVersion.
  • Geometry is stored as GeoJSON in NVARCHAR(MAX). Any geometry column is written only as WKT through STGeomFromText(@wkt).

Added in 0.3:

  • Tenant.BeaconProximityUuid UNIQUEIDENTIFIER NULL, with a unique constraint on (TenantId, BeaconProximityUuid) and a filtered unique index.
  • Venue.BoundaryGeoJson NVARCHAR(MAX) NOT NULL, ISJSON: the IMDF Venue polygon.
  • Beacon:
    • BeaconId BIGINT PK; TenantId, VenueId and LevelId foreign keys
    • X, Y DECIMAL(9,3)
    • ProximityUuid, with a composite foreign key to the tenant namespace
    • Major, Minor INT between 0 and 65535
    • IdentityScheme: a closed set, kept in the constants file
    • MeasuredPower, BeaconModelId, InstalledOn, BatteryServicedOn, IsActive
    • audit-stamped
    Indexes:
    • UNIQUE (ProximityUuid, Major, Minor): serves the publish-time collision check.
    • (TenantId, VenueId, LevelId, IsActive): serves editor loads and package builds.
    Deliberately excluded: key columns (keyed schemes get a separate encrypted table) and per-shopper sightings.
  • Sentinel: a fixed, mall-owned scanner. HmacSecret is encrypted at rest and serves the §3.17 ingest.
  • SentinelReadingHourly: MedianRssi and SampleCount per sentinel, beacon and hour.
    • High volume: about 5.3M rows per venue per year at 20 × 30 (arithmetic).
    • Kept hourly for 90 days, then as daily rows for 25 months.
    • Not audit-stamped per row (§3.6 noise filter).
  • On the device, per D6 (ruled 0.4):
    • Each venue version is one read-only SQLite file built by the publish engine. It holds only allow-listed tables, such as pkg_venue (including the boundary), pkg_level, pkg_unit, pkg_occupant, pkg_beacon and pkg_graph. Table names are provisional.
    • The file includes an FTS5 directory index and an R*Tree over unit bounds.
    • The routing graph is a CSR blob of Float32/Int32 arrays.
    • A separate small WAL database holds the package registry (installed versions, previous version kept until the new one verifies) and the telemetry queue.
    • No observed sightings are persisted, and no secrets are stored.
  • Shopper accounts (D1) 0.4: a shopper identity table separate from admin users. Columns are set when D1b fixes the sign-in method. It never stores position (invariant 4).
  • Added in 0.7 0.7. Names are provisional, and the schema is drafted in db/schema.sql:
    • Building (wings, annexes, parking structures) under a Venue. A Level belongs to a building, or is the venue's outdoor ground level, with an IsOutdoor flag (L30).
    • Unit.ParentUnitId for nested-facility interiors and sections, with a PassThrough flag (L29).
    • An AmenityType lookup, served through /api/lookups/:name (rule 5).
    • TransitPoint for boundary endpoints: train bridge, terminal, jeepney or taxi bay, parking entry (L30).
    • ObjectType, the global object library, and FloorObject, which holds each placement's width, depth, rotation and height in DECIMAL metres (L39).
    • NavConnector gains RiseM, InclineDeg and the elevator parameters (SpeedMps, DoorCycleS, ExpectedWaitS, the floors served) (L37, L38).
    • NavCorrection, the admin fixes to the auto-generated graph (L32).
    • VenueStatus, Closure and ShopperReport (moderated), for the live overlay (L28; R3).
    • BeaconPlan and BeaconPlanItem, the planner's outputs (L40).
    • AnalyticsConsent and pseudonymised RouteTrace, with a retention policy. Writes are gated by L42.
    The NavNode and NavEdge tables of 0.3 become publish-time outputs. The generated graph lives in the immutable package, and the database keeps only corrections.

Part 4: anchors

  • Anchors: 4.1 publish engine · 4.2 routing engine · 4.3 positioning · 4.4 package delivery & shopper shell · 4.5 venue boundary guard · 4.6 map editing (venue & level setup, map editor) · 4.7 directory & search · 4.8 shopper accounts · 4.9 live venue status · 4.10 BLE placement planner · 4.11 route analytics & consent · 4.12 notifications & promos. 0.6: 4.6–4.8 added and 4.4 renamed, closing C3. 0.7: 4.9–4.12 added; 4.6 now covers the whole site, the four-mode editor and graph generation; 4.3 gains the outdoor GNSS↔BLE handoff at entrances (L31). Every P4 and Beyond-P0 row cites an anchor.
  • 4.3's plausibility filter:
    • reject movement faster than walking speed along the walkway graph
    • require at least two consistent beacons, or one beacon plus a prior position
    • ignore identities that are in the namespace but not in the registry
    Its parameters come from Spike 1 and the prototype.
  • 4.3's live position engine (D4b, 0.5) 0.5:
    • One foreground SCAN_MODE_LOW_LATENCY scan with a single namespace ScanFilter, restarted at about 9 min, via Gabay's thin platform channel (0.6).
    • Native binary batches about every 100 ms.
    • Accelerometer steps with stillness freeze.
    • A compass gated at the WMM ±10% band.
    • A sparse fixed-lag Viterbi matcher on the CSR walkway graph, in the same worker isolate as A*.
    • Clamped per-model RSSI offsets.
    • Floor changes only at connector nodes.
    • Off-route hysteresis.
    • A confidence-gated dot, with fallback to "select where you are".
    Full evidence is in D4b.

6.10 Venue boundary and out-of-venue state (Priority: High)

Description. Gabay never shows a position it can't justify inside the venue that is open. When a scanned code belongs to another venue, or the position evidence falls outside the venue's boundary, the shopper sees a clear out-of-venue state instead of a guess. Traces to: shopper; PWD/senior shopper.

Acceptance criteria

  • 0.5 Choosing a place in "select where you are" puts the position at that place's entrance node on its floor, with an uncertainty halo. A later confident live fix supersedes it. No position is placed while the chosen place isn't in the open venue's published version.
  • 0.5 Picking a mall manually that isn't downloaded offers the download, and places no position until the package is verified.
  • QR criteria below apply only where a mall has installed optional QR signage (D4a).
  • Scanning a "You are here" code from a different venue shows "This code is for ⟨venue⟩ — Switch?" and places no position on the current map.
  • Scanning a code for a venue that isn't downloaded offers the download, and places no position until the package is verified.
  • Scanning a code whose anchor no longer exists in the current published version shows "This code is out of date — choose where you are" and places no position.
  • A position from any source that falls outside the boundary is never snapped to a walkway. The map shows "You're outside ⟨venue⟩" within one position cycle.
  • While the out-of-venue state is showing, the Start-route control is disabled and says why.
  • The decision is made on the device. No position or boundary event is sent to the server.

Out of scope: navigation beyond the venue boundary, including between venues. (0.7, L30: the boundary now covers the whole mall property, meaning its wings and buildings, outdoor grounds, parking and adjacent transit, so outdoor areas inside it are in scope. The 0.3 wording was "outdoor navigation to or between venues".)

Added to §6.9 Live position when it is written:

  • 0.5 With the app open inside a mall's boundary, the first live fix appears without any tap. The target time is set from Spike 1, since 1–3 s is UNVERIFIED. Until it appears, "select where you are" is offered.
  • 0.5 The dot follows the shopper at ≥1 Hz, snapped to walkways, and freezes when the shopper stands still. Beyond the uncertainty threshold it greys out, and then offers "select where you are".
  • 0.5 Every destination search defaults its origin to the live position when it is confident, otherwise to the last selected place.
  • 0.5 Floor changes are shown only after passing an escalator or lift connector.
  • A grey "Signal lost — you may have left ⟨venue⟩" after the configured number of cycles with no beacon from this venue.
  • An offer to switch when only beacons from a different Gabay venue are heard.
  • The out-of-venue state when a GNSS fix lands outside the boundary plus the buffer, within the accuracy limit (iOS).
  • No dot movement for observations implying movement faster than walking speed.
  • Identities in the namespace but not in the registry are ignored.
  • Mall admins see each beacon's last sentinel sighting and battery-service date, and a "Silent" state.

§7 accuracy caveat:

  • Live position engine accuracy (formerly "stage 2") is a pre-pilot target of 3–5 m off-peak. The prototype measures it first on budget phones. Accuracy at peak crowd density is unmeasured.
  • Published bounds: about 13–15 dB of loss from the user's own body, and about 5 dB from one person nearby.
  • No study comparing mall peak and off-peak was found UNVERIFIED.
  • No product copy states a numeric accuracy until the M6 measurement exists.
  • Outdoors (L31, 0.7): "GPS-enabled smartphones are typically accurate to within a 4.9 m (16 ft.) radius under open sky", and "their accuracy worsens near buildings, bridges, and trees" (GPS.gov). The sampled budget phones list no L5 band UNVERIFIED. Covered parking is treated as indoors (BLE).

§2 not-list:

  • No surveyed fingerprint positioning (stage 4). The costs:
    • About 20 s per survey point, or 28–56 collector-hours per mall on a 1 m grid.
    • 500–1,000 landmarks per mall, walked by paid collectors.
    • Accuracy fell from 2.4 m to 4.1 m after a year without maintenance (MLoc).
    • Vendors advise re-mapping when shelving or steel moves.
    • Revisit only if a client funds surveys and re-surveys.
  • No shopper-side positioning telemetry for beacon health. Mall-owned sentinels detect degradation instead. 0.7: the only shopper-side upload is the opt-in route analytics of L42, which stays behind its legal gate.
  • No evacuation responder location sharing, and no crowd data from phones (L27, L26; 0.7). Crowd-aware routing is listed under the §2 vision as a future plan fed by mall-owned sensors.

Standard fit

These are the places where Gabay strains the standard. Each goes to EXCEPTIONS.md or to section H.

  1. Public anonymous API. The standard assumes JWT on every route. Proposed: a read-only, published-only public surface. The package-field allow-list lint (0.3) gives this proposal the enforcer §13 requires.
  2. The local persistence library (D6, ruled 0.4): prebuilt SQLite with raw SQL through sqlite3 and better-sqlite3. Two libraries the mandate doesn't name need an owner note, and H5 still applies. E-01 is withdrawn.
  3. The Firebase backend (L98, 0.42): PostgreSQL in place of MSSQL, pg in place of mssql, Firebase Auth in place of jsonwebtoken + bcryptjs, and the firebase-admin and firebase-functions libraries. §1 denies PostgreSQL by name and names the lead as the approver of every deviation. The product owner approved E-06 to E-09 without the lead; the P0 gate's "every §1 deviation … has written lead sign-off" item is therefore open until Allan or Raphael countersigns. Raw parameterized SQL and the no-ORM rule are kept.
  4. Three shopper-app packages §1 does not name (L99, 0.43): speech_to_text, mobile_scanner and audioplayers, brought in by the Gabay Demo build of 2026-10-06. EXCEPTIONS E-10 to E-12, approved by the product owner like E-06 to E-09; the lead's countersignature is open. The voice itself goes through Gabay's own platform channel, like the BLE layer (L23), so no package; shared_preferences is named by the standard's §4.
  5. A mobile shopper app alongside a web admin: a device matrix for every positioning change, and the iOS build compiling on every PR.
  6. Editor saves versus the audit trail: the Batched Save Rule.
  7. The canvas editor versus FieldSpec: the Panel Rule.
  8. Privacy by architecture: the Device-Only Position Rule.
  9. Deny-list scope for on-device persistence: H5.
  10. When the §2 stubs exist versus C.1 Phase 0: H1.
  11. Phase 2 forms before Phase 3 machinery: H2.
  12. §5 "all ticked" versus "at first need": H3.
  13. §12 counted as eleven versus twelve: H4 (erratum).
  14. §14.2 skills versus the carve-out: H6.
  15. Blueprint Parts 4–6 and PRD §8–§9 never scheduled: H7.

The section H test, applied to G1–G6 (0.3): all six gaps are domain-specific, so they stay Gabay-local. They are recorded as declined proposals:

  • G1: a domain enforcer.
  • G2: a preference, with no incident and no enforcer.
  • G3: an exclusion.
  • G4: a product decision.
  • G5 and G6: domain rules.
