L1012026-10-06QR scan: a hard reset of the live position and a calibration point for the phone; codes for entrances and connectors with placement advice; Spike 1 measures the gain 0.45The product owner shared a note on what QR scanning gives an indoor positioning system: a ground-truth anchor that resets the position estimate (at entry, where beacon positioning is weakest; at junctions and landmarks against accumulated drift; at floor exits, where beacons alone tell floors apart badly), and a calibration point, since at the moment of a scan the phone's position is known and what it hears can be compared with what it should hear, giving a per-device RSSI offset for the rest of the session. They asked whether the plan covered this.
  • What stood before: D4a and L16 keep optional QR codes that give "an exact spot and floor"; L99 added a place code at every door. But a scanned place was only a fallback origin: "a later confident beacon fix supersedes it" (D4a), "a later confident live position replaces a selected one" (PRD §6.8). D4b item 7 had per-model RSSI offsets "plus clamped online self-calibration" with no trigger named. D4b item 8 handles floor changes by connector-node hysteresis. Spike 1 has a QR-scan test (C12a) that checks scanning works, not what it buys. Nothing advised where codes go. So the position-and-floor part was covered; the reset, the calibration trigger and placement were not.
  • Rulings (one round, 2026-10-06):
    1. Hard reset: a scan re-seeds the matcher at the code's spot and floor. The beacon track continues from there with minimal uncertainty, and the floor state is set, not inferred. Anchor codes and place codes alike. This amends D4a's wording for scanned codes; a place chosen by search, description or floor pick is still superseded by a confident live fix. Not taken: reset only when the dot is unsure or greyed; keep the scan as a fallback origin.
    2. Calibration, saved on the phone: at a scan the per-device offset is computed, clamped, averaged with earlier scans, kept across sessions with the L88 settings and never sent; it is re-estimated at every scan. Not taken: session only (the note's wording), which throws the result away; no scan trigger.
    3. Placement advice: publish emits codes for entrances, kiosks and every floor's lift, escalator and stair landings as well as shops and amenities, and the publish screen's print sheet lists those spots first with the reason (fewest beacons heard; floor ambiguity). Printing any of them stays the mall's choice (PRD §6.8). Not taken: emit the codes with no advice; shops and amenities only.
    4. Spike 1 measures the gain: at the penthouse marks, per phone, the offset a scan yields against the Samsung reference, and the dot error over the following 30 s with and without the reset and the offset. The spike session implements it inside the existing QR test (L49 scope). Not taken: measure at the pilot.
  • Mechanism (Blueprint §4.3):
    • Reset: the matcher's state collapses to the code's node (an anchor's exact spot; a place code's door node) on the code's floor, with the code's uncertainty: about 1 m for an anchor and about 2 m for a door code (DESIGN CHOICE, tuned in Spike 1; alternative: one radius for both). The item-8 floor window is set to that floor. The PositionSource reports live-confident from that moment, so L99's plain wording applies.
    • Calibration: the beacons heard in about 2 s around the scan are compared with the path-loss model's expected RSSI at the known spot, from the package's beacon positions and RF parameters (TX power, RSSI at 1 m, wall loss: the L58 model). The median difference, clamped to item 7's band (−3 to +10 dB in Google's Exposure Notifications table; the clamp is ±10 dB around the per-model seed), is the sample. Samples are averaged, weighted by the number of beacons heard, saved on the phone and applied to every RSSI afterwards. No fingerprint survey is needed: stage 4 stays excluded (PRD §2). DESIGN CHOICES: median over mean (robust to one shadowed beacon); the 2 s window; beacon-count weighting. Alternatives: mean; a single reading; equal weights.
    • Codes and the print sheet: publish already derives gabay://<venue>/<place> for every place (L99); L101 includes entrances and connector landings (one code per connector per floor it stops at) and adds a print sheet (PDF) ordered by recommendation. Optional signage; no code is required (PRD §6.8).
    • Invariant 4: the offset is a device value, not a position. It never leaves the phone.
  • Risks (RECALLED reasoning, to be measured in Spike 1): entrances hear the fewest beacons, so the first offset is noisy (the clamp and re-estimation bound the damage); a shopper holding the phone up to a wall shadows the beacons behind them, which can bias the sample (the median helps; the test measures it); a shopper scanning from 2–3 m away is placed at the code, within the reset radius; a moved or spoofed code that contradicts strong beacon evidence by more than the plausibility limit (4.3 filter) is flagged to the mall rather than trusted (DESIGN CHOICE; alternative: trust the scan always).
  • Expectations: no table or column; a device-side offset saved with the settings; two more publish outputs (connector and entrance codes, the print sheet); the Spike 1 QR test grows two measurements. Accuracy and the floor handoff improve wherever a mall prints codes, at no hardware cost; where it prints none, nothing changes.
  • Risks: the gain is unmeasured until Spike 1 reports; a mall may print no codes, so the engine must stand without them (it does: D4b). The print sheet is one more admin screen in P0-03.
Product ownerMatches the recommendation on all four pointsThe product owner's note and interview, 2026-10-06; D4a, D4b items 7 and 8, L16, L40 (entrance beacons), L58 (the RF model), L99; Google's Exposure Notifications calibration table (cited at item 7); PRD §2 (stage 4 excluded), §6.8PRD 0.12 §6.3, §6.8, §6.9; Blueprint 0.11 §4.1, §4.3; FEATURE_PIPELINE 0.10 (P0-03, P0-08, P0-13); the D4a and D4b notes above; the Spike 1 QR test (gabay_spike, the spike session)

  • How the demo handles it: its four venues are Dart code. The app makes no network call and loads no file or JSON for a venue; a change to any venue is a new APK. Nothing here carries over to the product; the demo stays the reference for look and behaviour (L85).
  • What already stood in the product: the mall's staff edit a draft in the editor (P0-11); publish validates it and builds an immutable, versioned package, a SQLite file with the graph as a CSR blob (D2, D6, P0-03), stored in Cloud Storage (L98); the shopper app downloads it, verifies the SHA-256, checks for a newer version by ETag "when a newer version exists and the phone is online" (PRD §6.4), and swaps only after verifying (verify-then-swap; a shopper on the previous version keeps it, PRD §6.3); a published version is never edited (the Frozen Package rule); closures and out-of-service connectors ride a live overlay without a republish (L28, PRD §6.14); the 2.5D renderer draws from the package's geometry (L83). So "every map comes from the database" was already the design. Open were the refresh cadence, how far the overlay reaches, who edits, and proof at mall scale.
  • Is there a better approach? Four alternatives were weighed and passed over: a live map read from the API on every open (no offline, which D2 requires; more load; nothing immutable to verify); a real-time document store syncing map data (Firestore or the Realtime Database are excluded by L98's one-relational-database rule); hand-maintained map files in storage (IMDF or GeoJSON) with no editor, validation or versioning, which is what the package already is, except generated and validated by publish; and server-rendered map tiles (online only, no offline routing). The package model keeps offline use, immutability, validation and a cheap refresh. Traced to D2, D6, L98.
  • Rulings (one round, 2026-10-07):
    1. Refresh: while the app is online and in the foreground (invariant 5), it checks for a newer version on launch, when it detects or the shopper picks the venue, and on a timer, default 5 minutes, a setting. A newer package downloads in the background and replaces the old one only when no route or evacuation is active; a shopper mid-route finishes on the version they started with, and the sheet then says "Map updated". Offline shoppers keep what they have. Not taken: on launch only, as written; a push notice with an immediate swap (no shopper push channel exists before P3, and a route would change under the shopper).
    2. Overlay scope, confirmed: only store status and closures reach shoppers without a republish (L28). Names, categories, logos, hours and geometry change only through a new published version, so the map, the search index (FTS5 in the package) and the route always agree, offline included. A tenant swap is a publish, which reaches phones by ruling 1. Not taken: store text in the overlay (the package's index and labels would disagree with it until the next publish); promos and announcements in the overlay (D1/P3 ties promos to signed-in, opted-in shoppers).
    3. Who edits: Gabay draws the pilot mall's map in the editor together with the mall's staff, which also exercises the editor; the mall's staff then maintain it with the L41 roles (Mall admin publishes; Venue editors draft). Later malls onboard themselves, with CAD/IMDF import (P1-03) to shorten the drawing. Not taken: malls self-serve from day one (the editor would have to be polished for untrained staff at P0); Gabay maintains every map as a service (Gabay's staff become the bottleneck).
    4. Seed an invented mall: P0-02 gains a second seed, an Aurora-sized invented mall (about 400 places; a cross-shaped galleria on five levels with two basements and a car park), re-created as data from the mock-up's venue description and published through P0-03 like any mall. This proves publish, download, refresh and routing at mall scale before the pilot, and gives the shopper app a showcase. Not taken: the penthouse only (the first mall-scale run would be the pilot); all four demo venues (the three kit venues would need their own converter).
  • Mechanism (Blueprint §4.4): the refresh check is one conditional request for the venue's package with If-None-Match set to the held version's ETag; 304 means nothing to do. The interval is GlobalSetting package.refreshCheckS, default 300 (rule 5). The download goes to a staging file, is verified against the SHA-256 in the version record, and is swapped in by the shell when no route or evacuation is active; the routing isolate reopens the new file. The "Map updated" note is a DESIGN CHOICE (alternative: a silent swap; passed over because a shopper may notice a changed store list and wonder). The overlay (§4.9) is unchanged. Invariants 4 and 5 are untouched: the check sends no position and runs only in the foreground.
  • The seed: the demo's tool/dump_venue.js already emits the mock-up's venue as JSON (test/golden/venue.json) for its parity test. The product seed uses a converter from that description into the editor's model (units, openings, connectors with heights, amenities, transit points), written as a seed (§8.2), then published. This is data, not code: L34 bans copying code, and no Dart or JavaScript from the demo is reused. DESIGN CHOICE: a converter from the JSON over drawing the mall by hand in the editor (hours of clicks, and the demo's geometry would drift). Its name stays Aurora Mall: invented, like the rest.
  • The standard: no new package, table or column. The refresh interval is a setting row; the seed is a seed file. The Frozen Package rule, the Public-Read rule and the Sealed Package rule are unchanged.
  • DESIGN CHOICES beyond the rulings: the 5-minute default (alternative: 15 minutes, fewer requests, slower updates; tuned at the pilot); swapping only between trips rather than at the next launch (alternative: next launch, simpler, slower); the converter as the seed source (above).
  • Open questions: none new. OQ1 (scale) gains the refresh check's load: one small request per online shopper per interval, served from the version index.
  • Expectations: a mall-owner change is live for online shoppers within one interval of publishing, without an app update. P0-02 grows from S to M for the second seed and its converter. P0-04 gains the timer and the between-trips swap. OQ1's load estimate gains one request per shopper per 5 minutes.
  • Risks: a mall that publishes often makes shoppers download often (packages are 0.3–3 MB gzipped, unverified until measured; the pilot measures the real size and the publish rate); a long trip delays the swap until it ends, so a closure published mid-trip still reaches the shopper through the overlay, which is the overlay's job; the converter is throwaway tooling that must not become a second way to author venues (it writes the seed once).

  • How the demo finds the nearest (demo_app lib/state/app_state.dart 254–332, lib/venue/graph.dart 189–199): it snaps the dot to the straight-line nearest walkway node on its floor (skipping store-interior nodes), runs one Dijkstra to every node, and caches the result keyed by that node, wheelchair mode, shortcuts, the avoid set and the closures version.   • Risks: the pilot waits for iOS if OQ13 slips; Android 7 phones have older Bluetooth stacks, so the test set needs one (OQ15); a whole-graph sweep of about 20k nodes on a budget phone is unmeasured (Spike 2 measures it; DC12's throttle is the fallback).

L1052026-10-07Visit forecasts: an in-house seasonal model, run nightly, for mall visits per day, peak hours, store visits per week and real footfall from the mall's door counters; it learns from holidays, paydays, mall events and weather, and is shown only once proven 0.47The product owner, on the merchant question: "option 1, and also add a forecast regarding the user volume, and other relevant and feasible data, which infers from reliable data set like previous activities, manners, graphs, real days significance like holidays, etc".
  • Checked today: §1 denies Python backends, so the usual Python forecasting libraries are out. BigQuery ML ARIMA_PLUS (docs.cloud.google.com, CREATE MODEL for time series, and ML.FORECAST): hourly or daily data, a built-in holiday calendar with "PH : Philippines", custom holidays, and a forecast range at a chosen confidence level. Its cost was not checked.
  • Stated to the product owner: the dashboard counts opted-in shoppers, so app "volume" is not mall footfall. Real footfall needs the mall's own door counters.
  • Rulings (one round, 2026-10-07):
    1. Engine: in-house, in the nightly Node function, as recommended. Not taken: ARIMA_PLUS (a new service and an EXCEPTIONS entry, cost unchecked, and it too must see a holiday before learning it); in-house now and BigQuery after a year.
    2. Forecasts: all four offered: mall visits per day (14 days), visits by hour (7 days), store visits per week (4 weeks, DESIGN CHOICE), and real footfall per day from the mall's CSV uploads.
    3. Signals: all four offered: PH holidays (a table Gabay staff update yearly; rule 5), paydays (DC16), mall events entered by the admin, and weather and typhoon signals. Weather was presented as the least feasible: an outside feed, unvetted, known only days ahead (OQ17).
    4. Reliability: shown only once proven, as recommended: 8 weeks of history and a back-test error within a limit (OQ19), always with its range, its error and "based on shoppers who opted in". Not taken: shown from week 1 with wide ranges.
  • Mechanism (Blueprint §4.13): a same-weekday (and hour) baseline over 8 weeks; one multiplicative effect per signal, learned from its past days, and not applied until seen twice; the range from the errors of re-forecasting the last 4 weeks (WAPE). Each step is a DESIGN CHOICE with its alternative named there (Holt-Winters or ARIMA in Node).
  • Gate: app-visit forecasts read the L104 roll-up and wait for the L42 gate. The footfall forecast uses no shopper data and is not gated.
  • The standard: rule 5 (holidays and events are tables with screens); rule 1's no-float rule (forecasts are DECIMAL); no new package or service.
  • Open questions: OQ17 (weather feed: PAGASA or a paid API, its terms, licence and cost), OQ18 (whether the pilot mall has counters, and their export), OQ19 (the error limit).
  • Expectations: P2-04 (L) is new. Nothing shows until 8 weeks after app figures start, and a holiday's effect applies only after it has been seen twice, so the first year shows few holiday effects.
  • Risks: small opt-in counts make app forecasts noisy, and at a small mall they may never pass the error limit; a weather feed may cost money or bar commercial use; holidays moved by proclamation need yearly upkeep.

L1082026-10-07Draft maps generated from uploaded plans, by rules with no AI: the admin sets buildings and levels, uploads a plan per level, and reviews and corrects an unpublished draft before publishing 0.49The product owner: "for the virtual map, is this feasible[:] the admin upload selects how many buildings then how many floors or levels each[,] then each file will be uploaded per each designated distinction[,] then the ai will generate a virtual map that matches the standard model[;] that will be the initial unpublished one and the admin can review and edit it", then "can it be done without ai, instead by using an algorithm or a logic matching or even surpassing the capabilities".
  • Answered first: feasible, and it fits the model already planned (P0-01 buildings and levels; FloorUnderlay and the UNDERLAY / CAD_IMPORT files; drafts and publish, L102; the walkway graph generated at publish, L32). Draft quality depends on the input: CAD and IMDF convert exactly, vector PDFs well, scans moderately, directory-board photos poorly. Rules beat AI on CAD, IMDF and vector PDF (exact, repeatable, explainable, nothing leaves the browser) and match it on clean scans. Reading names from a scan needs OCR, which is itself a neural network (RECALLED); an outside AI service would send confidential drawings away (DPA, DPO, an exception).
  • Rulings (two rounds, 2026-10-07):
    1. Inputs: DXF and IMDF, vector PDF, and scanned or raster plans. Directory-board photos stay underlay-only.
    2. Where it runs: in the admin's browser, as recommended; the draft is saved, and the original only if the admin chooses (DC21). Not taken: the server (function limits with one instance; drawings on our servers).
    3. Tier: P1, widening P1-03, as recommended. Gabay draws the pilot mall with its staff anyway (L102). Not taken: P0; vector at P0 and scans at P1.
    4. Review gate: every generated element confirmed before publish, as recommended. Not taken: low-confidence only; one check per floor.
    5. Names on scans: typed or picked by hand from the store list, as recommended; no OCR, so strictly no AI.
    6. Assisted tools: none for now; the three offered (magic-wand room fill, snap to detected walls, click a gap to make a door) are drafted for a later revision as P3-04.
    7. Floor alignment: two shared points clicked on each level, as recommended. Not taken: matching shapes automatically.
    8. No spike first: the product owner's choice; recommended was a throwaway spike on the penthouse drawing (L48) and one mall plan, measuring minutes to a publishable draft. The requirement is written now, and accuracy is learned while building.
  • Mechanism (Blueprint §4.6): DXF and IMDF entities read directly; vector PDF line paths and text; scans scaled by two points, thresholded and deskewed, walls as thick strokes, doors as wall gaps of 0.7–1.5 m confirmed by a swing arc, rooms as regions closed by walls with the largest as the concourse. Escalator direction, lift details, walk-through marks and beacons are never generated. Every generated element is saved with NeedsReview and a DraftConfidence; publish refuses while any is unreviewed.
  • The standard: db/schema.sql 0.10 adds the two columns and a partial index to Unit, Opening, FloorObject and NavConnector (DC22; alternative: one review table). Limits are settings (import.*). The parsing and image libraries are OQ20: each needs a pin, and an EXCEPTIONS entry if §1 does not name it.
  • Amends: PRD §6.22's "the uploaded file is kept as a CAD_IMPORT file" becomes "kept only if the admin chooses" (DC21).
  • Expectations: P1-03 grows (L) and gains a review list in the editor; P3-04 is drafted; later malls onboard from their own plans.
  • Risks: without a spike, scan accuracy is unknown until it is built, and a poor result costs reviewers time; very large scans may be slow in the browser (capped by a setting); libraries outside §1 add exceptions.

    1. L1292026-10-07S5 hook rulings: a commit that touches a surface bumps its patch version; the verify Stop hook requires a run on the current code and shows a failure rather than trapping the session; it runs for the main session only 0.64S4 closed (dod-reviewer's final verdict "acceptable"; phase-reports/phase-1/S4.md, 397bf1d). Briefing S5 (hooks and npm run verify) found three points the standard, the plan and WORKING_AGREEMENT.md §6 do not settle, put to the product owner, 2026-10-07:
  • Rulings (all as recommended):
    1. Version bump: standard §8.1 says pre-commit "bumps only the surface(s) the commit touched" but not by how much. Each commit touching the shopper app or the admin page raises that surface's patch (0.1.0 → 0.1.1), and the top changelog entry's version and date are stamped to match; minor and major are raised by hand, and the hook keeps a hand-raised version instead of bumping it again. Not taken: build number only (drifts from §8.1's "bumps"); minor per commit.
    2. Verify Stop hook: it blocks until npm run verify has run on the current code. If that run failed, the session may stop, and the product owner sees a "verify FAILED" notice. So an agent can still stop to ask a question mid-fix, and a stopped database cannot trap the session in a loop. Not taken: strict green-or-no-stop (§6's literal reading); block once (the main session's first design, which let a session stop without ever running verify).
    3. Scope: the verify Stop hook runs for the main session only. Coders hand back to the main session, which re-runs the checks and cannot finish without verify. Not taken: every subagent, since parallel coders would each rebuild gabay_dev and break each other's runs.
  • DESIGN CHOICES (main session), within the approved plan: api-coder builds S5 from the main session's design (the plan names the main session; L122 puts well-defined coding on Sonnet; S4 set the same precedent), and the main session reviews and re-runs every check. Alternative: the main session builds it. Shared code (app/lib/core, app/lib/shared, l10n) counts as touching both surfaces. app/pubspec.yaml's flutter create default 1.0.0+1 becomes 0.1.0+1 and is not stamped (one Flutter project carries two version streams; builds pass --build-name and --build-number). Alternative: stamp the shopper stream into it.
  • Expectations: S5 closes when each hook is shown blocking a bad sample and passing a good one, and npm run verify ends ALL GREEN.
  • Risks: verify runs for minutes (Flutter tests, two schema runs, the seed, the emulator); a red run can still be left behind, but never silently.

  • Rulings (all as recommended):
    1. Protection model: every change goes on a branch and a pull request into main; lint and e2e must pass; "Do not allow bypassing" is on, so the product owner and the agents acting as them cannot push untested code to main.   • Risks: more steps per change and a CI wait of roughly 6–15 minutes per pull request; a pending pull request blocks nothing else, since work continues on branches.

Closing assessment

The largest remaining risk is that invariant 4 is undefined at its edge. "Shopper position never leaves the device unless aggregated above a minimum-count threshold" doesn't say whether venue-level presence counts as position.

  • Aggregation necessarily happens after data leaves a single device. So any shopper-side upload, including D3's P1 aggregated analytics (the feature mall clients pay for), conflicts with the literal wording.
  • The 0.3 pass worked around this for positioning health by using mall-owned sentinels. Analytics, though, can't be designed until the invariant is defined.
  • It isn't closed here because it's a preserved invariant. Defining it needs the product owner, the DPO and likely counsel (NPC Advisory 2025-02 draws the line between anonymisation and pseudonymisation).
  • Runner-up: there's no pilot venue, so every field claim stays UNVERIFIED until M6.
